Joshua Hahn

12 items
Patches Submitted (3)
Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-21 Last comment 2026-11-02 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v7 0/5] mm/page_counter: move stock from mem_cgroup to page_counter 5 patches
Show 5 individual patches
  • [1/5] mm/memcontrol: flatten try_charge_memcg control flow Submitted 2026-10-21
  • [2/5] mm/page_counter: introduce per-CPU stock Submitted 2026-10-21
  • [3/5] mm/page_counter: make page_counter_try_charge() stock-aware Submitted 2026-10-21
  • [4/5] mm/memcontrol: move memory stock to page counters Submitted 2026-10-21
  • [5/5] mm/memcontrol: add stock to the memsw page counter Submitted 2026-10-21
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series moves the stock management from mem_cgroup to page_counter, allowing each page_counter to have its own per-CPU cache of pre-charged pages. This change is necessary for future work on scalable and performant page_counters, such as the tiered memcg limits series. The decoupling of memory and memsw stocks in cgroup v1 users may lead to different sizes of precharged caches, but the patch preserves the invariant that memory.usage_in_bytes is less than or equal to memory.memsw.usage_in_bytes by reporting the smaller value. This change has no functional impact on memcg v2 users.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Michal Hocko Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Michal Hocko requested a high-level design overview of the patch in the cover letter, specifically asking about locking and cache implications at different hierarchy levels. He also questioned how the invariant that memory.memsw.usage_in_bytes is larger than or equal to memory.usage_in_bytes can be broken when counters are always charged together.
Show original comment 
[Sorry for coming to this really late]

On Wed 21-10-26 15:55:09, Joshua Hahn wrote:
> v6 --> v7
> =========
> - Preserve two cgroup v1 proprerties that previously followed implicitly
>   from sharing a stock:
>   - memory.usage_in_bytes <= memory.memsw.usage_in_bytes
>   - Reclaim does not swap when memory and memsw have equal limits.
> - Wordsmithing commit messages for 4/5 and 5/5 to document the
>   cgroupv1 divergence (bounded) between memory and memsw caused by
>   independent stock.
> 
> INTRODUCTION
> ============
> Memcg keeps a per-CPU stock of precharged pages so that small, frequent
> allocations do not walk the page_counter hierarchy every time.
> Today, the stock implementation is within memcontrol code, even though
> the operation it caches is a page_counter charge. This makes it
> difficult to add new page_counters to a memcg and preserve the fast
> path behavior.
> 
> This matters for future work like my tiered memcg limits series [1]
> which introduces multiple new page_counters to memcg. Without making
> stock a page_counter-level property, it means that every memcg charge
> now goes through multiple page_counter hierarchy walks, instead of
> being able to cache these charges.
> 
> To make future page_counters scalable and performant, move stock from
> mem_cgroup to page_counter so that each page_counter can opt into its
> own per-CPU cache of pre-charged pages.

What does it mean that each counter can opt into its own per-cpu
caching?

> We get an added benefit of simplifying try_charge_memcg(), which now
> has all the stock management handled transparently within the
> page_counter layer.

This is quite a large change and I believe it will be really benefitial
to have some high level design overview in the cover rather than trying to
chase it in respective patches.
Namely, are there any changes to the locking (which is arguably quite
tricky to allow completely lockless fast path)? Also what are
implications to caching at different levels of the hierarchy? An example
of cache propagation up/down the hierarchy during
charge/uncharge/draining would be really useful to wrap ones head around
how this is supposed to work.

> EFFECT ON MEMCG V2 USERS
> ========================
> This series has no functional changes intended for memcg v2 users. We
> preserve all draining, refilling, and (un)charging behavior, including
> the uncharge path's refills / direct uncharges.
> 
> EFFECT ON MEMCG V1 USERS
> ========================
> For memcg v1 users, the decoupling of the memsw and memory stock means
> that each of them now manage their own independent stocks and can lead
> to a different size of precharged cache for each.
> 
> Cgroup v1 has an invariant that memory.memsw.usage_in_bytes is larger
> than or equal to memory.usage_in_bytes, because memsw is a superset of
> memory. With separate stocking, this could have been broken in scenarios
> where the memory stock is bigger than the memsw stock, leading to
> memory usage appearing to be inflated and greater than memsw usage,
> even though the real usage preserves the invariant.

How can this happen when counters are always charged together?

-- 
Michal Hocko
SUSE Labs

> So right now for cgroupv2, there's kind of a 1-1 mapping between
> page_counter and memcg. I say "kind of" because there still is a
> page_counter for swap, but it takes a completely different charging
> path (i.e. not try_charge_memcg).
> 
> This means that currently stocking charge for memcg is equivalent to
> stocking charge for the memcg->memory page_counter.

correct

> However, one of the series that I'm currently working on [1] adds new
> page_counters to struct memcg to track the usage of memory
> per-memcg, per-tier. The goal is to (1) track how much memory of a
> cgroup lives on which tier, and (2) to limit the usage so that a single
> cgroup doesn't use up all the fast tier memory alone, and shares it
> fairly with all the other cgroups on the host.

Right. I guess you have layed down your motivation quite well in your
original cover letter.

> When adding a new per-tier page_counter to struct mem_cgroup that gets
> charged for every memcg_charge, I cannot use the stock fastpath since
> the stock does not distinguish where the memory resides. It would
> essentialy boil down to this pattern:
> 
> try_charge_memcg
>    \__ page_couner_try_charge(memcg->tier[0])
>    \__ consume_stock()
> 
> Where every path still has to do a hierarchy charge to the tier it's
> charging to, whereas this patch gets us:
> 
> try_charge_memcg
>    \__ page_counter_try_charge(memcg->tier[0])
>           \__ consume_stock(memcg->tier[0])
>    \__ page_countertry_charge(memcg->memory)
>           \__ consume_stock(memcg->memory)
> 
> So that we can avoid the hierarchy walk for most charges.
> 
> I'd be happy to submit a new cover letter which better explains this
> idea!

I guess I still do not understand what you mean by opting-in to the
caching. I believe you should focus less about what you plan to do and
rather explain what _this_ patchset achieves and how it works.
Also focus on describing why do we need to keep caches on all layers of
the hierarchy.

> > > We get an added benefit of simplifying try_charge_memcg(), which now
> > > has all the stock management handled transparently within the
> > > page_counter layer.
> > 
> > This is quite a large change and I believe it will be really benefitial
> > to have some high level design overview in the cover rather than trying to
> > chase it in respective patches.
> 
> Sounds good with me. I can add the call traces above which should
> explain my thought process a bit more. Hopefully the problem I am
> trying to solve is evident there.

I believe I follow where you are heading. I am not sure this is the
right path but that is a separate discussion. I would focus on whether
moving cache to the counter makes sense on its own (without that future
work) at this stage. Quite honestly I am still not convinced this move
is the right thing but I would like to fully understand the mechanics
first. Having discrepancies among different counters just sounds like a
potential source of future problems.

On Mon, 26 Oct 2026 10:03:58 +0100 Michal Hocko <mhocko@suse.com> wrote:

> [Sorry for coming to this really late]

Hi Michal, no worries at all : -) Thanks for taking a look!

> On Wed 21-10-26 15:55:09, Joshua Hahn wrote:
> > v6 --> v7
> > =========
> > - Preserve two cgroup v1 proprerties that previously followed implicitly
> >   from sharing a stock:
> >   - memory.usage_in_bytes <= memory.memsw.usage_in_bytes
> >   - Reclaim does not swap when memory and memsw have equal limits.
> > - Wordsmithing commit messages for 4/5 and 5/5 to document the
> >   cgroupv1 divergence (bounded) between memory and memsw caused by
> >   independent stock.
> > 
> > INTRODUCTION
> > ============
> > Memcg keeps a per-CPU stock of precharged pages so that small, frequent
> > allocations do not walk the page_counter hierarchy every time.
> > Today, the stock implementation is within memcontrol code, even though
> > the operation it caches is a page_counter charge. This makes it
> > difficult to add new page_counters to a memcg and preserve the fast
> > path behavior.
> > 
> > This matters for future work like my tiered memcg limits series [1]
> > which introduces multiple new page_counters to memcg. Without making
> > stock a page_counter-level property, it means that every memcg charge
> > now goes through multiple page_counter hierarchy walks, instead of
> > being able to cache these charges.
> > 
> > To make future page_counters scalable and performant, move stock from
> > mem_cgroup to page_counter so that each page_counter can opt into its
> > own per-CPU cache of pre-charged pages.
> 
> What does it mean that each counter can opt into its own per-cpu
> caching?

So right now for cgroupv2, there's kind of a 1-1 mapping between
page_counter and memcg. I say "kind of" because there still is a
page_counter for swap, but it takes a completely different charging
path (i.e. not try_charge_memcg).

This means that currently stocking charge for memcg is equivalent to
stocking charge for the memcg->memory page_counter.

However, one of the series that I'm currently working on [1] adds new
page_counters to struct memcg to track the usage of memory
per-memcg, per-tier. The goal is to (1) track how much memory of a
cgroup lives on which tier, and (2) to limit the usage so that a single
cgroup doesn't use up all the fast tier memory alone, and shares it
fairly with all the other cgroups on the host.

When adding a new per-tier page_counter to struct mem_cgroup that gets
charged for every memcg_charge, I cannot use the stock fastpath since
the stock does not distinguish where the memory resides. It would
essentialy boil down to this pattern:

try_charge_memcg
   \__ page_couner_try_charge(memcg->tier[0])
   \__ consume_stock()

Where every path still has to do a hierarchy charge to the tier it's
charging to, whereas this patch gets us:

try_charge_memcg
   \__ page_counter_try_charge(memcg->tier[0])
          \__ consume_stock(memcg->tier[0])
   \__ page_countertry_charge(memcg->memory)
          \__ consume_stock(memcg->memory)

So that we can avoid the hierarchy walk for most charges.

I'd be happy to submit a new cover letter which better explains this
idea!

> > We get an added benefit of simplifying try_charge_memcg(), which now
> > has all the stock management handled transparently within the
> > page_counter layer.
> 
> This is quite a large change and I believe it will be really benefitial
> to have some high level design overview in the cover rather than trying to
> chase it in respective patches.

Sounds good with me. I can add the call traces above which should
explain my thought process a bit more. Hopefully the problem I am
trying to solve is evident there.

> Namely, are there any changes to the locking (which is arguably quite
> tricky to allow completely lockless fast path)?

No changes to locking. In fact this series is mostly just moving code
from mm/memcontrol.c to mm/page_counter.c, everything is essentially
the same. 

> Also what are implications to caching at different levels of the
> hierarchy? An example of cache propagation up/down the hierarchy during
> charge/uncharge/draining would be really useful to wrap ones head around
> how this is supposed to work.

This series should not have any changes for cgroup v2, including when
and where the cache is stocked / drained in the hierarchy. I can
include a snippet on how stocking currently works to add a visual
explanation, if that helps too.

> > EFFECT ON MEMCG V2 USERS
> > ========================
> > This series has no functional changes intended for memcg v2 users. We
> > preserve all draining, refilling, and (un)charging behavior, including
> > the uncharge path's refills / direct uncharges.
> > 
> > EFFECT ON MEMCG V1 USERS
> > ========================
> > For memcg v1 users, the decoupling of the memsw and memory stock means
> > that each of them now manage their own independent stocks and can lead
> > to a different size of precharged cache for each.
> > 
> > Cgroup v1 has an invariant that memory.memsw.usage_in_bytes is larger
> > than or equal to memory.usage_in_bytes, because memsw is a superset of
> > memory. With separate stocking, this could have been broken in scenarios
> > where the memory stock is bigger than the memsw stock, leading to
> > memory usage appearing to be inflated and greater than memsw usage,
> > even though the real usage preserves the invariant.
> 
> How can this happen when counters are always charged together?

Yeah, so this part is a little bit annoying. Let's just look at a single
leaf cgroup for example, where the limit for memsw and memory are both
64 pages.

charge_memcg(64)
	raw memory: 64			raw memsw: 64
	reported memory: 64		reported memsw: 64
	memory stock: 0			memory stock: 0

swap out 32 pages
	raw memory: 32			raw memsw: 64
	reported memory: 32		reported memsw: 64
	memory stock: 0			memsw stock: 0

free 32 pages
	raw memory: 0			raw memsw: 32
	reported memory: 0		reported memsw: 32
	memory stock: 0			memsw stock: 0

charge_memcg(1)
	raw memory: 1			raw memsw: 33
	reported memory: 64		reported memsw: 33
	memory stock: 63		memsw stock: 0

Where the last charge_memcg() causes the inversion. memsw tries to
charge a batch of 64 to refill the stock, but fails since 32+64=96
puts the memsw over its max limit. So it falls back to direct charging
where it charges the page_counter directly, and ends up with 0 stock
and reporting a usage of 33.

On the other hand, memory will greedily refill the stock with 64 pages
and since usage is 0, the batch refill will succeed, and it will report
a usage of 64.

So the idea with reporting the minimum is to report the minimum of the
two reported values, we will just fuzzily report the stocked value.
IIRC usage_in_bytes has always been fuzzy anyways, due to how much
memory has been stocked.

Thanks again for taking a look, Michal! I hope you have a great day : -)
Joshua

> -- 
> Michal Hocko
> SUSE Labs

[1] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260807202059.2620949-1-joshua.hahnjy@gmail.com/

Reading my original cover letter again, yeah. It does feel a bit strange
to call it "opt-in"... I guess I just meant that not all page_coutners
will be getting it, it would only really be the memory page_counter
(and memsw page_counter for cgroup v2). I'll change the wording in the
next version.

> I believe you should focus less about what you plan to do and
> rather explain what _this_ patchset achieves and how it works.
> Also focus on describing why do we need to keep caches on all layers of
> the hierarchy.

Yes, I'll do that. Actually, this patchset is really quite limited in
what it does. I actually explicitly tried to do no optimizations or
behavioral changes at all, this is mostly just a clean-cut code
move from memcg code to page_counter code. 

You may recall earlier versions of this series did a bunch more stuff
(making the stock scale past 7 memcgs, dynamically allocating the stock,
at some point I even had a version that only cached leaf cgroups, etc.)

I got some feedback that these optimizations were orthogonal to the
movement of stock from memcg to page_counter, which is why this version
explicitly does none of the fancy optimizations on top. It mostly is
just moving code from memcg to page_counter.

> > > > We get an added benefit of simplifying try_charge_memcg(), which now
> > > > has all the stock management handled transparently within the
> > > > page_counter layer.
> > > 
> > > This is quite a large change and I believe it will be really benefitial
> > > to have some high level design overview in the cover rather than trying to
> > > chase it in respective patches.
> > 
> > Sounds good with me. I can add the call traces above which should
> > explain my thought process a bit more. Hopefully the problem I am
> > trying to solve is evident there.
> 
> I believe I follow where you are heading. I am not sure this is the
> right path but that is a separate discussion. I would focus on whether
> moving cache to the counter makes sense on its own (without that future
> work) at this stage.

If I'm being honest, most of the generic benefits that exist beyond that
work were in the other part of this series that I got rid of to scale
down the scope of this version. But to explicitly name the generic
benefits, it would be unbounded memcg stock caching per-cpu, eliminating
random evictions, (like you mentioned above as well) limiting stocks
to leaf memcgs only, etc.

I think there was a proposal to make the number of memcg stocks per cpu
even lower than it already is, because the author wanted to include
additional fields into the stock that would make 7 no longer fit into
a single cacheline. I feel like we are reaching some limits with that
side as well, so the "unbounded" portion seems to be quite opportune.

But I believe those benefits that we would like to see are made better
by this series, which makes the stocking transparent such that the new
optimizations don't make the existing memcg charging code any more
complex than it is. By abstracting away this mechanism, we can make sure
that memcg just handles the logical charging semantics, and page_counter
handles the implementation-level details of stock management.

Personally I am a big fan of how try_charge_memcg looks after this
series, what do you think? 

> Quite honestly I am still not convinced this move
> is the right thing but I would like to fully understand the mechanics
> first. Having discrepancies among different counters just sounds like a
> potential source of future problems.

I hope that I can present a good argument for why this is not as
concern as it may seem initially.

My first point is that the memory usage counters were always fuzzy, as
specified in the memcg v1 documentation too: 

usage_in_bytes ... doesn't show 'exact' value of memory (and swap)
usage, it's a fuzz value for efficient access.

Secondly, the raw value of the usage is actually always accurate. It is
just the stock portion that overcharge the memory at times, making it
seem like there is a discrepancy. Flushing the memcg stock immediately
brings the counters to their real values (like what happens right now).

Finally, this is a change that is only limited to cgroup v1 users.
This entire series actually has no effect at all on cgroup v2 users,
which I think is a nice perk. Obviously I'm not out here trying to
make cgroup v1 unusable, but I wonder if anyone is still using the
legacy feature.

I hope this wasn't too much of a brain dump on you. Thanks for your
feedback Michal. Please let me know if there is anything I can do to
clarify my intent.

Would you prefer if we continud the discussion here, or if I sent out
a new series with just the cover letter changed? (I don't anticipate
any code changes, based on sashiko review or anything else that I see).

Thanks again. I hope you have a great day!
Joshua

> limiting cache to leaf memcgs makes a lot of sense and addresses a big

It's not a big change, actually. This was the implementation from the
origianl RFC of the series:

https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260410210742.550489-8-joshua.hahnjy@gmail.com/

The idea is that you treat a cgroup as a leaf until it becomes a parent,
at which point you drain the stock. Stock can land on the parent due
to hierarchy walks, but I can also write another part to this that
prevents stock from getting cached when a cgroup has children.

> > I think there was a proposal to make the number of memcg stocks per cpu
> > even lower than it already is, because the author wanted to include
> > additional fields into the stock that would make 7 no longer fit into
> > a single cacheline. I feel like we are reaching some limits with that
> > side as well, so the "unbounded" portion seems to be quite opportune.
> >

> > by this series, which makes the stocking transparent such that the new
> > optimizations don't make the existing memcg charging code any more
> > complex than it is. By abstracting away this mechanism, we can make sure
> > that memcg just handles the logical charging semantics, and page_counter
> > handles the implementation-level details of stock management.
> 
> Yes, these are all good arguments. Especially when clarified that this
> leaf only feature. I have to say I was struggling to see how caching on
> all levels of the hierarchy would work in practice.

Sounds good. If it makes sense to you then, I can fold in the

> > Personally I am a big fan of how try_charge_memcg looks after this

> that for memsw counter altogether. Fewer problems to deal with, explain

I think the problem actually still exists, unfortunately. In fact I
think the issue becomes bigger if you remove stocking for memsw.

Let's say only memory gets stock. Then on a single page charge for a
new cgroup:

memory page_counter charges 64 pages to refill the stock, memsw only
charges 1 page to fulfill the current allocation. Then you have
memory: 64 pages
memsw: 1 page

And the invariant that memory <= memsw is immediately broken.

Unfortunately only stocking for memsw is not a solution as well since
successful memsw charges still charge memory and will therefore go
through a hierarchy walk.

Actually maybe then, the solution is just to remove stocking for
cgroup v1 users? Does that sound like a fair tradeoff to you?

> > This entire series actually has no effect at all on cgroup v2 users,
> > which I think is a nice perk. Obviously I'm not out here trying to
> > make cgroup v1 unusable, but I wonder if anyone is still using the
> > legacy feature.

> > clarify my intent.

> > a new series with just the cover letter changed? (I don't anticipate
> > any code changes, based on sashiko review or anything else that I see).

Sounds good to me. Thanks!

Signals: acknowledged need for clarification, agreed to provide additional information
Joshua Hahn (author) Neutral LLM
Author addressed Michal Hocko's concern about the potential source of future problems due to discrepancies among different counters, explaining that the memory usage counters have always been fuzzy and that the stock portion can overcharge memory at times. Author agreed with Michal's suggestion to remove stocking for memsw counter altogether if there is a way to opt-in to caching, but ultimately decided to keep it as it is. A new cover letter will be sent out soon.

Last comment 2026-11-05 [PATCH v8 0/7] mm/page_counter: move stock from mem_cgroup to page_counter 7 patches
Show 7 individual patches
  • [1/7] mm/memcontrol: flatten try_charge_memcg control flow Neutral Last comment 2026-11-05 Balbir Singh
  • [2/7] mm/page_counter: introduce per-CPU stock
  • [3/7] mm/page_counter: make page_counter_try_charge() stock-aware
  • [4/7] mm/memcontrol: check PF_MEMALLOC after trying the limits
  • [5/7] mm/memcontrol: move memory stock to page counters
  • [6/7] mm/memcontrol: add stock to the memsw page counter
  • [7/7] mm/memcontrol: restrict memory stock to leaf cgroups on v2
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series moves the stock implementation from memcontrol to page_counter, allowing for better separation of charge caching and policy management. The stock is a per-CPU cache of precharged pages that speeds up small allocations by avoiding frequent walks of the page_counter hierarchy. By moving it to page_counter, the patch simplifies the handling of stock in non-leaf cgroups and prepares for future improvements. It also ensures that locking behavior remains unchanged and that exact charging is preserved for non-spinning callers.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Gave Acked-by

Show original comment 
It's not a big change, actually. This was the implementation from the
origianl RFC of the series:

https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260410210742.550489-8-joshua.hahnjy@gmail.com/

The idea is that you treat a cgroup as a leaf until it becomes a parent,
at which point you drain the stock. Stock can land on the parent due
to hierarchy walks, but I can also write another part to this that
prevents stock from getting cached when a cgroup has children.

---

Sounds good. If it makes sense to you then, I can fold in the
leaf-only caching into this series to maintain more parity with the

---

I think the problem actually still exists, unfortunately. In fact I
think the issue becomes bigger if you remove stocking for memsw.

Let's say only memory gets stock. Then on a single page charge for a
new cgroup:

memory page_counter charges 64 pages to refill the stock, memsw only
charges 1 page to fulfill the current allocation. Then you have
memory: 64 pages
memsw: 1 page

And the invariant that memory <= memsw is immediately broken.

Unfortunately only stocking for memsw is not a solution as well since
successful memsw charges still charge memory and will therefore go
through a hierarchy walk.

Actually maybe then, the solution is just to remove stocking for
cgroup v1 users? Does that sound like a fair tradeoff to you?

Last comment 2026-11-04 [PATCH 0/2] arm64: sdei: Handle exceptions taken inside the event handler 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] arm64: sdei: Run the event handler on the kernel vectors Neutral Last comment 2026-11-04 Usama Arif
  • [2/2] arm64: sdei: Preserve the interrupted context's exception registers
Neutral LLM

The patch series addresses an issue where SDEI events can take exceptions inside their handlers, causing context loss and CPU stalls. Patch 1 runs the event handler on kernel vectors to prevent this, while Patch 2 saves and restores exception registers around the handler to preserve the interrupted context.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Usama Arif ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) Neutral LLM
Raised a concern that the patch may not cover all possible exception paths, specifically mentioning __sdei_handler() calls to irqentry_nmi_enter() and irqentry_nmi_exit(). Suggested swapping the exception handling in __sdei_handler() around the NMI entry and exit pair.
Show original comment 
On Wed,  4 Nov 2026 18:09:33 +0000 "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org> wrote:

> With KPTI, VBAR_EL1 holds the trampoline vectors from the return to EL0
> until the next exception from EL0 maps the kernel and installs the
> kernel vectors. The trampoline vectors only handle exceptions from EL0;
> their current-EL half is zero-filled.
> 
> An SDEI event is dispatched regardless of PSTATE.DAIF, so it lands there
> whenever the CPU is in user space. The SDEI entry trampoline maps the
> kernel but leaves VBAR_EL1 alone. An exception taken by the handler then
> executes the zeros, takes an undefined instruction exception to the same
> vector, and loops there forever. The event never completes and the CPU
> is lost; RCU stall reports are the first sign of it.
> 
> RAS notifications land in user space whenever a task consumes the
> error, and NMI backtraces land wherever the CPU happens to be. The GHES
> handler takes no exception on its own, but any exception in the handler
> path does it: a WARN, a kprobe hit, or a fault fixed up by a _nofault()
> accessor.
> 
> Install the kernel vectors in do_sdei_event() when VBAR_EL1 holds the
> trampoline vectors, and restore it before returning to firmware,
> including when the handler fails. The IRQ-like resume into user space
> keeps using the interrupted value, as it must go through the trampoline
> to map the kernel.
> 
> Fixes: 79e9aa59dc29 ("arm64: sdei: Add trampoline code for remapping the kernel")
> Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
> Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
> ---
>  arch/arm64/kernel/sdei.c | 22 ++++++++++++++++++++--
>  1 file changed, 20 insertions(+), 2 deletions(-)
> 
> diff --git a/arch/arm64/kernel/sdei.c b/arch/arm64/kernel/sdei.c
> index 778f2a1faac8..9fa2a3aeed48 100644
> --- a/arch/arm64/kernel/sdei.c
> +++ b/arch/arm64/kernel/sdei.c
> @@ -18,6 +18,7 @@
>  #include <asm/sections.h>
>  #include <asm/stacktrace.h>
>  #include <asm/sysreg.h>
> +#include <asm/vectors.h>
>  #include <asm/vmap_stack.h>
>  
>  unsigned long sdei_exit_mode;
> @@ -214,6 +215,20 @@ unsigned long __kprobes do_sdei_event(struct pt_regs *regs,
>  	if (arm64_kernel_unmapped_at_el0())
>  		clobbered_registers++;
>  
> +	/*
> +	 * With KPTI, VBAR_EL1 holds the trampoline vectors from the return to
> +	 * EL0 until the next exception from EL0 maps the kernel. They only
> +	 * handle exceptions from EL0: an exception taken by the handler would
> +	 * run their empty current-EL half and never come back. Run the handler
> +	 * on the kernel vectors. The IRQ-like resume below keeps using the
> +	 * interrupted VBAR, so it still goes through the trampoline.
> +	 */
> +	if (arm64_kernel_unmapped_at_el0() &&
> +	    vbar == (unsigned long)this_cpu_read(this_cpu_vector)) {
> +		write_sysreg((unsigned long)vectors, vbar_el1);
> +		isb();
> +	}
> +

Does this cover the whole path that can take an exception?  The
swap is done inside do_sdei_event(), but __sdei_handler() calls
irqentry_nmi_enter() before it and irqentry_nmi_exit() after it,
and both of those still run with the trampoline vectors in
VBAR_EL1 when the event interrupted user space.

Both can take an exception.  ct_nmi_enter() opens with
WARN_ON_ONCE(ct_nmi_nesting() < 0), and ct_nmi_exit() has
WARN_ON_ONCE(ct_nmi_nesting() <= 0) and
WARN_ON_ONCE(!rcu_is_watching_curr_cpu()).  A WARN is a BRK, so
either would run the zero filled current-EL half and lose the CPU
in the way the commit message describes.

Would doing the swap in __sdei_handler(), around the whole NMI
entry and exit pair, close that window?


>  	/* Retrieve the missing registers values */

Signals: WAITING_FOR_REVIEW
Last comment 2026-11-05 [PATCH v2 0/2] arm64: sdei: Handle exceptions taken inside the event handler 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] arm64: sdei: Run the event handler on the kernel vectors Neutral Last comment 2026-11-05 Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Catalin Marinas
  • [2/2] arm64: sdei: Preserve the interrupted context's exception registers Neutral Last comment 2026-11-05 Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Breno Leitao
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses an issue where SDEI events can cause exceptions in the event handler, leading to unexpected behavior and crashes. The patches modify the SDEI event handling code to run the handler on kernel vectors instead of trampoline vectors, which are not suitable for synchronous exceptions. Additionally, the patches save and restore exception registers around the event handler to prevent overwriting of interrupted context's registers. This change improves the robustness of SDEI event handling and prevents issues such as CPU stalls, SIGILL, and SIGSEGV in user tasks.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Positive LLM
The author acknowledged that the IRQ-like resume into user space must still go through the trampoline to map the kernel, and confirmed that the patch series addresses the issue of exceptions taken by the handler breaking the interrupted context.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>

An SDEI event can land anywhere, and a synchronous exception taken by its
handler breaks the context it interrupted:

 - With KPTI, an event that lands in user space runs the handler on the
   trampoline vectors, whose current-EL half is empty. The exception
   loops there and the CPU is lost.

 - The exception overwrites ELR, SPSR, ESR and FAR. If the event landed
   in exception entry or return, the interrupted context runs on the
   handler's values: a task gets SIGILL, or the kernel oopses on a fault
   it never took.

Patch 1 runs the handler on the kernel vectors. Patch 2 saves and
restores the exception registers around it.

Handlers take such exceptions in normal operation now that SDEI delivers
NMI backtraces: show_regs() reads through _nofault() accessors that
fault, and a kprobe or WARN anywhere in the path takes a BRK.

Tested under QEMU TCG with TF-A, 4 CPUs, 20 x sysrq-l under load, with a
test-only copy_from_kernel_nofault() of address 0x10 in
sdei_nmi_handler():

 - v7.4-rc1, KPTI on: CPUs stuck in the trampoline vectors, RCU stalls.
 - v7.4-rc1, KPTI off: 60 "unsafe: exception during handler" warnings,
   SIGILL and SIGSEGV in user tasks, and an oops in execve().
 - With the series, KPTI on and off: no warnings, no stalls. The same
   with an eprobe on irq_disable that reads address 0x10, which faults
   inside irqentry_nmi_enter() when the event lands in user space.

An earlier revision of patch 2 was also run on arm64 hardware with
firmware SDEI, where a plain sysrq-l was enough to trigger the warning
without it.

Changes since v1
================

  https://lore.kernel.org/all/20261104180936.2981070-1-kas@kernel.org/

 - Switch VBAR and save the exception registers in __sdei_handler(),
   before irqentry_nmi_enter(), and restore them after
   irqentry_nmi_exit(). Both can take an exception too: the eprobe above
   wedged v1 with KPTI on (Usama). do_sdei_event() takes the interrupted
   VBAR for the IRQ-like resume.

Catalin Marinas Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that irqs_disabled() may be false in __this_cpu_read() due to preempt debugging and pseudo-NMIs, which would cause a warning, and suggested using raw_cpu_read() or saving/restore PMR and priorities similar to kernel_entry code.
Show original comment 
Sashiko had a comment that the above should be raw_cpu_read() since with
preempt debugging and pseudo-NMIs, irqs_disabled() is false here and the
check in __this_cpu_read() will warn.

Thinking about it, further down on the irqentry_nmi_enter() ->
lockdep_hardirqs_off() we'll hit the irqs_disabled() issue again. We
could just do a raw_local_irq_save/restore() around this function.
Unnecessary for DAIF but useful with pseudo-NMIs.

Alternatively, __sdei_asm_handler() could save/restore PMR and change
the priorities similar to the kernel_entry code. Actually, this may be
better since do_sdei_event() also checks regs_irqs_disabled() and we
don't have any regs->pmr populated.

Worth a separate patch to fix SDEI handling with pseudo-NMI on and no
changes to __this_cpu_read() above needed. So, for this patch:

Signals: requested changes, alternative solutions
[PATCH] mm/compaction: sample empty pageblocks again from a random offset Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch modifies the Linux kernel's compaction algorithm to sample empty pageblocks from a random offset when they are found to be empty. This is done to mitigate aliasing issues, where fixed layouts can cause the free scanner to miss free pages. The change allows for more accurate detection of free memory and prevents stalls in direct reclaim and huge page faults.

[PATCH] mm/memory: refuse to map page cache folios that are not uptodate Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch prevents the Linux kernel from mapping page cache folios that are not uptodate, which can leak stale memory to userspace. The patch adds a function `file_folio_not_uptodate` to check if a folio is uptodate and refuses the mapping if it's not. This change affects the `finish_fault` and `do_set_pmd` functions in the kernel's memory management code. The goal of this patch is to prevent silent leaks of stale memory, which can occur when userspace maps page cache folios that have not been properly read in or cleared.

[PATCH v2 0/3] mm: make watermark checks honour ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] mm: page_alloc: add a watermark check that knows the migratetype
  • [2/3] mm: page_alloc: make the high-order watermark check honour ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT
  • [3/3] mm: vmscan: tell direct reclaim the allocation is ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses a performance issue in Linux kernel's memory management under certain conditions. When defrag_mode is enabled, high-order watermark checks incorrectly count free pages of all migratetypes, leading to inefficient compaction and direct reclaim. The patches modify the watermark check to consider only the request's migratetype and skip free lists that cannot be used for allocation. This change improves performance by reducing compaction stalls and 64K faults under memory pressure.

  • [07/18] mm: free the pages waiting for a grace period before isolation fails
  • [08/18] mm/compaction: free the migrated folios that wait for a grace period
  • [09/18] mm/memory-failure: handle folios with PG_rcu_free
  • [10/18] mm: have folio_mark_accessed() return whether the folio is hot
  • [11/18] mm/filemap: mark a folio accessed in one place in filemap_read()
  • [12/18] mm/filemap: set PG_rcu_free on the folios that reads keep coming back to
  • [13/18] mm/filemap: read from the page cache without folio references
  • [14/18] mm/filemap: read large folios without folio references
  • [15/18] mm/filemap: don't store f_ra.prev_pos for FMODE_RANDOM files
  • [16/18] mm/shmem: count accesses to a folio the way filemap_read() does
  • [17/18] mm/shmem: read without folio references
  • [18/18] mm/filemap: add a debugfs switch for lockless page cache reads
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series proposes a change to how Linux handles page cache reads, allowing multiple CPUs to read from the same folio without taking a reference. This is done by introducing a new flag PG_rcu_free and deferring the freeing of folios until an RCU grace period. The goal is to reduce contention between readers and improve performance in scenarios where many threads are reading the same pages. The patch series includes 18 patches that implement this change, including modifications to filemap_read(), shmem, and other related functions.

10 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about how to handle PG_rcu_free flag in the presence of multiple readers accessing the same file, explaining that it's set under a reference and cleared only by the free, allowing lockless lookups to read until rcu_read_unlock().
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

Reading a page cache folio without taking a reference would keep CPUs
that read the same file from bouncing its refcount.  But without a
reference the folio can be freed, and its memory reused, while the
reader copies from it.

Add PG_rcu_free: the free of a folio with the flag waits for an RCU
grace period.  A reader that saw the flag under rcu_read_lock(), and
then still found the folio where it looked, can read it until
rcu_read_unlock().  A split copies the flag, as such a reader may still
be reading the new folios.

The folios wait on per-CPU lists, one grace period in flight per CPU:
call_rcu() per folio would queue a callback for every folio of a
truncated file.  A work, which can reschedule, frees each batch.
Reclaim depends on these frees, so the workqueue is WQ_MEM_RECLAIM.

The flag is 64-bit only (CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE): page flags are scarce
on 32-bit, and the contention this avoids is a problem of large
machines.  It is off on PREEMPT_RT, where expedited grace periods are
normal ones, so the waiting pages cannot be freed quickly when memory
runs short.

pgfree_rcu in /proc/vmstat counts the deferred pages.  Nothing sets the
flag yet.

Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: rework for flagging at read time, 64-bit only and not PREEMPT_RT,
 shorter comments and changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+config PAGE_RCU_FREE
+	def_bool y
+	depends on 64BIT
+	depends on !PREEMPT_RT
+	help
+	  Reads from the page cache can copy from folios that are read
+	  again and again without taking a reference on them, so small
+	  reads of one file from many CPUs do not contend on the folio's
+	  reference count.  The cost is that memory freed from such
+	  folios becomes reusable only after an RCU grace period.

+/*
+ * Each CPU has at most one grace period in flight (@queued), covering
+ * @batch.  Folios freed meanwhile wait on @pending for the next one.
+ */
+struct rcu_free_pcpu {
+	spinlock_t lock;
+	bool queued;
+	struct list_head pending;
+	struct list_head batch;
+	struct rcu_work rwork;
+};
+
+static DEFINE_PER_CPU(struct rcu_free_pcpu, rcu_free_pcpu);
+
+static struct workqueue_struct *rcu_free_wq __ro_after_init;
+
+static void rcu_free_start(struct rcu_free_pcpu *r)
+{
+	lockdep_assert_held(&r->lock);
+
+	if (r->queued || list_empty(&r->pending))
+		return;
+
+	list_splice_init(&r->pending, &r->batch);
+	r->queued = true;
+	WARN_ON_ONCE(!queue_rcu_work(rcu_free_wq, &r->rwork));
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_pages(struct list_head *list)
+{
+	struct folio_batch fbatch;
+	struct folio *folio, *next;
+
+	folio_batch_init(&fbatch);
+	list_for_each_entry_safe(folio, next, list, lru) {
+		if (!folio_batch_add(&fbatch, folio)) {
+			free_unref_folios(&fbatch);
+			cond_resched_tasks_rcu_qs();
+		}
+	}
+	if (folio_batch_count(&fbatch))
+		free_unref_folios(&fbatch);
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_workfn(struct work_struct *work)
+{
+	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = container_of(to_rcu_work(work),
+					       struct rcu_free_pcpu, rwork);
+	LIST_HEAD(done);
+
+	spin_lock_irq(&r->lock);
+	list_splice_init(&r->batch, &done);
+	r->queued = false;
+	rcu_free_start(r);
+	spin_unlock_irq(&r->lock);
+
+	rcu_free_pages(&done);
+}
+
+static void rcu_free_defer(struct folio *folio)
+{
+	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = raw_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu);
+	unsigned long flags;
+
+	spin_lock_irqsave(&r->lock, flags);
+	list_add_tail(&folio->lru, &r->pending);
+	rcu_free_start(r);
+	spin_unlock_irqrestore(&r->lock, flags);
+}
+
+static void __init rcu_free_init(void)
+{
+	int cpu;
+
+	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE))
+		return;
+
+	rcu_free_wq = alloc_workqueue("rcu_free",
+				      WQ_MEM_RECLAIM | WQ_PERCPU, 0);
+
+	for_each_possible_cpu(cpu) {
+		struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = per_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu, cpu);
+
+		spin_lock_init(&r->lock);
+		INIT_LIST_HEAD(&r->pending);
+		INIT_LIST_HEAD(&r->batch);
+		INIT_RCU_WORK(&r->rwork, rcu_free_workfn);
+	}
+}
+

 		unsigned int order, fpi_t fpi_flags)
 {
@@ -1327,6 +1417,19 @@ static __always_inline bool __free_pages_prepare(struct page *page,

+	/*
+	 * Not for a hwpoisoned order-0 page: it never reaches the allocator,
+	 * and page_handle_poison() takes a reference right after this free.
+	 */
+	if (unlikely(folio_test_rcu_free(folio)) &&
+	    !(PageHWPoison(page) && !order)) {
+		VM_WARN_ON_ONCE(fpi_flags & FPI_NOLOCK);
+		__folio_clear_rcu_free(folio);
+		count_vm_events(PGFREE_RCU, 1 << order);
+		rcu_free_defer(folio);
+		return false;
+	}
+
 	trace_mm_page_free(page, order);
 	kmsan_free_page(page, order);
 
@@ -6401,6 +6504,8 @@ void __init setup_per_cpu_pageset(void)
 	for_each_online_pgdat(pgdat)
 		pgdat->per_cpu_nodestats =
 			alloc_percpu(struct per_cpu_nodestat);
+

 }

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing a concern about the reuse of page cache folios in fuse and virtio consoles, specifically that stolen pipe buffers are reused without being freed. The author agrees to remove the ->try_steal function from page_cache_pipe_buf_ops, which will prevent this issue.
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

PG_rcu_free holds back the reuse of a folio's memory until a grace
period after the folio is freed.  A stolen page cache pipe buffer is
reused without being freed: remove_mapping() takes the folio out of the
page cache and the caller keeps it.  fuse_try_move_folio() puts it into
a fuse file, where writes change it under a reader that found it before
the steal.

Drop ->try_steal from page_cache_pipe_buf_ops.  fuse_try_move_folio()
and virtio_console's pipe_to_sg() then copy, as they already do when
stealing fails.  SPLICE_F_MOVE from the page cache into a fuse file or
a virtio console now copies.

Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: rewrote the changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledging a fix is needed, agreement to restructure
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
Author addressed a concern that fuse_try_move_folio() should handle folios marked PG_rcu_free by copying them instead of refusing them, explaining that this is already the behavior for other cases where fuse cannot take a folio.
Show original comment 
fuse_try_move_folio() takes the folio of a pipe buffer and puts it into
a fuse file, where writes change it.  A folio with PG_rcu_free may still
be copied by a lockless page cache read that found it before it left
the page cache.

Such a folio reaches fuse when a mapped file page is gifted with
vmsplice(SPLICE_F_GIFT) and its file is then truncated.
fuse_check_folio() refuses it only because the flag is not among those
it allows, and with a dump_page().

Copy such a folio instead, as fuse does whenever it cannot take one.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the visibility of deferred folios in memory statistics, explaining that they have refcount 0 and are not on free lists, thus missing from all LRU counts and free counts. The author added counters for NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES to /proc/vmstat and show_free_areas() to account for these pages.
Show original comment 
Deferred folios have refcount 0 and are not on the free lists, so no
counter shows them.  Memory that waits for a grace period is missing
from the free count and from every LRU count.

Count it per zone in NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES: nr_free_rcu in /proc/vmstat,
free_rcu in show_free_areas().  Preparation for freeing the waiting
pages sooner when they hold back memory that is needed.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

Signals: clarification, explanation
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author acknowledged that a CPU freeing many flagged folios at once can hold a lot of memory, and agreed to restructure the code in v2 to take both lists when more than 4 MiB is pending on a CPU, and only expedite the grace period when the zone is below its high watermark or when the pages waiting in it are more than an eighth of its free pages. The author also explained that the expedited grace period interrupts every CPU.
Show original comment 
From: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>

A deferred folio waits one to two grace periods on its CPU's list, where
the page allocator cannot use it.  A CPU that frees many flagged folios
at once, for example when a file is truncated, can hold a lot of memory
there.

When more than 4 MiB is pending on a CPU, a work takes both of the CPU's
lists and frees them after an expedited grace period.  That grace period
starts after the take, so it covers every reader that found one of the
folios.  The task that frees them does not wait.

An expedited grace period interrupts every CPU, so keep it for when the
memory matters.  That is when the zone is below its high watermark, or
when the pages waiting in it are more than an eighth of its free pages.
The watermark alone comes too late for a bulk free.  A truncate or
fadvise(POSIX_FADV_DONTNEED) of a hot range defers all of it while the
zone still has memory.  When the readers that refill the range then run
short, nothing is freed that would check again.

With rcu_normal, the expedited grace period is a normal one.

pgfree_rcu_expedite in /proc/vmstat counts the expedite runs that freed
something.

Signed-off-by: Linus Torvalds <torvalds@linux-foundation.org>
[kas: take both lists, expedite only when the memory matters, rewrote
 the changelog]
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/* Free what rcu_free_take() took: the grace period starts after that */

+static void rcu_free_expedite_workfn(struct work_struct *work)
+{

+					       expedite);

 static void rcu_free_defer(struct folio *folio)
 {
 	struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = raw_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu);
 	struct zone *zone = folio_zone(folio);
+	unsigned long free, waiting;

 	rcu_free_start(r);
 	spin_unlock_irqrestore(&r->lock, flags);
+
+	/* Unlocked: a stale count delays the expedite by one free */
+	if (data_race(r->nr_pending) <= RCU_FREE_EXPEDITE ||
+	    work_pending(&r->expedite))
+		return;
+
+	/*
+	 * An expedited grace period interrupts every CPU: only while the
+	 * zone is short of memory, or while more than an eighth of what it
+	 * has free waits here.
+	 */
+	free = zone_page_state(zone, NR_FREE_PAGES);
+	waiting = zone_page_state(zone, NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES);
+	if (free < high_wmark_pages(zone) || waiting > free / 8)
+		queue_work(rcu_free_wq, &r->expedite);
 }

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged a technical issue, agreed to restructure
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed the concern that reclaim fails to free waiting pages when they and other reclaimable pages would bring an eligible zone above its minimum watermark, agreeing to restructure in v2 to add a new function, rcu_free_drain(), which frees these pages straight to the buddy lists.
Show original comment 
When reclaim fails, should_reclaim_retry() looks only at free and
reclaimable pages.  It can hand the allocation to the OOM killer while
enough memory is about to come back from the pages that wait for a
grace period.

Before should_reclaim_retry() gives up, free the waiting pages if they
and what reclaim can still free would bring an eligible zone above its
min watermark, and retry.  The drain frees them straight to the buddy
lists, where the retry's watermark check sees them, and waits for the
expedites already in flight.  That costs an expedited grace period,
which is cheap next to the OOM killer.  The retries are bounded per
no-progress streak, so a drain that does not help still ends in an OOM
kill.

pgfree_rcu_drain in /proc/vmstat counts the drains that freed something.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/*
+ * Free what every CPU's lists hold, straight to the buddy lists: the
+ * callers want to allocate the pages next.  A concurrent drain holds the
+ * lock until it has freed what it took.
+ */
+static void rcu_free_drain(void)
+{
+	static DEFINE_MUTEX(lock);
+	LIST_HEAD(list);
+	int cpu;
+
+	mutex_lock(&lock);
+	for_each_possible_cpu(cpu) {
+		struct rcu_free_pcpu *r = per_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu, cpu);
+
+		spin_lock_irq(&r->lock);
+		rcu_free_take(r, &list);
+		spin_unlock_irq(&r->lock);
+	}
+	if (rcu_free_flush(&list, true))
+		count_vm_event(PGFREE_RCU_DRAIN);
+	/* An expedite in flight holds pages it took off the lists */
+	for_each_possible_cpu(cpu)
+		flush_work(&per_cpu_ptr(&rcu_free_pcpu, cpu)->expedite);
+	mutex_unlock(&lock);
+}
+
 static void __init rcu_free_init(void)
 {
 	int cpu;

 	return !!__gfp_pfmemalloc_flags(gfp_mask);

+/*
+ * Reclaim is about to give up: if the pages waiting for a grace period,
+ * with what reclaim can still free, would bring an eligible zone above its
+ * min watermark, free them and retry.  Not for a dying task, which frees
+ * memory by exiting.
+ */
+static bool rcu_free_wait(gfp_t gfp_mask, unsigned int order,
+			  struct alloc_context *ac, int alloc_flags,
+			  int *rcu_free_waits)
+{
+	struct zone *zone;
+	struct zoneref *z;
+
+	if (!IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE))
+		return false;
+	if (tsk_is_oom_victim(current) || fatal_signal_pending(current))
+		return false;
+	if (*rcu_free_waits >= MAX_RECLAIM_RETRIES ||
+	    order > PAGE_ALLOC_COSTLY_ORDER)
+		return false;
+
+	for_each_zone_zonelist_nodemask(zone, z, ac->zonelist,
+				ac->highest_zoneidx, ac->nodemask) {
+		unsigned long waiting, available;
+
+		if (cpusets_enabled() && (alloc_flags & ALLOC_CPUSET) &&
+		    !__cpuset_zone_allowed(zone, gfp_mask))
+			continue;
+
+		waiting = zone_page_state_snapshot(zone, NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES);
+		if (!waiting)
+			continue;
+
+		available = waiting + zone_reclaimable_pages(zone) +
+			    zone_page_state_snapshot(zone, NR_FREE_PAGES);
+		/*
+		 * Count pages only: for order > 0 __zone_watermark_ok() also
+		 * wants a free block of that order, and the waiting pages are
+		 * not free yet.
+		 */
+		if (__zone_watermark_ok(zone, 0, min_wmark_pages(zone),
+					ac->highest_zoneidx, alloc_flags,
+					available)) {
+			(*rcu_free_waits)++;
+			rcu_free_drain();
+			return true;
+		}
+	}
+
+	return false;
+}
+

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged fix needed
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about the interaction between memory hotplug and RCU free pages, explaining that alloc_contig_range() and offline_pages() migrate folios out of a range but then check if every page is free, which fails when a migrated folio has PG_rcu_free. The author proposed to free waiting folios before checking, allowing alloc_contig_range() to find the start of the free range again.
Show original comment 
alloc_contig_range() and offline_pages() migrate the folios out of a
range and then check with test_pages_isolated() that every page is free.
A migrated folio that had PG_rcu_free waits for a grace period after its
free, so the check fails.  alloc_contig_range() returns -EBUSY, and a
CMA or gigantic page allocation can fail; offline_pages() retries until
the grace period ends.

When the check fails and the zone has pages waiting, free them first.
alloc_contig_range() then finds the start of the free range again, as
the freed folio can merge with a free buddy below it.
offline_pages() does so only after a pass that migrated folios: an
offline stuck on a busy page would otherwise start an expedited grace
period on every retry.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+		/* A freed folio can merge with a free buddy below start */
+		outer_start = find_large_buddy(start);
+		ret = test_pages_isolated(outer_start, end, mode);
+	}
+
+	if (ret) {
 		ret = -EBUSY;
 		goto done;
 	}

+void rcu_free_sync(void);

+/* Whether @zone has pages waiting for a grace period */
+static inline bool zone_rcu_free_waiting(struct zone *zone)
+{
+	return IS_ENABLED(CONFIG_PAGE_RCU_FREE) &&
+	       zone_page_state_snapshot(zone, NR_FREE_RCU_PAGES);

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, explanation
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed the concern that compaction fails more often when migrating blocks containing PG_rcu_free folios, which are freed after a grace period. The author agreed to restructure the code in v2 to free pages waiting for a grace period before draining per-cpu lists and checking if compaction is done.
Show original comment 
A block that compaction has migrated every folio out of becomes free
once those folios are back in the buddy lists.  A migrated folio that
had PG_rcu_free comes back only after a grace period, so compaction
finds the block still in use, migrates more blocks than it needs and
fails more often.  defrag_mode relies on compaction making whole free
blocks, and there it shows up as allocation stalls.

When compaction moves past a block it migrated from and the zone has
pages waiting, free them before it drains the per-cpu lists and checks
whether it is done.  The drain costs an expedited grace period, so
async compaction, which must not block, skips it.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

 				/* No more flushing until we migrate again */
 				last_migrated_pfn = 0;

2.54.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged fix needed, agreed to restructure
Kiryl (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about memory_failure() and soft_offline_page() handling pages waiting for an RCU grace period, agreeing to free such pages first in get_any_page() and before setting PG_hwpoison, and also adding a synchronize_rcu() call after returning success. The patch will need further revision.
Show original comment 
A page whose free waits for an RCU grace period has refcount 0 and is
neither on the LRU nor in the buddy allocator.  memory_failure() and
soft_offline_page() cannot handle such a page: get_any_page() retries a
few times and gives up.  Soft offline of the page fails, and a hard
error is ignored.  If the page is part of a large folio, the folio is
later freed with the poisoned page in it, and that page can be handed
out again.

Free the waiting pages first when the page looks like one of them, and
again on each retry in get_any_page(): the page can be freed while it
retries.  memory_failure() has to do it before it sets PG_hwpoison.  The
free of a poisoned order-0 page drops the page instead of returning it
to the buddy allocator, so a later drain would still leave the error
ignored.

A folio with PG_rcu_free that is still in use may be copied by a reader
that holds no reference.  When memory_failure() succeeds, its caller can
unmap the page from the direct map under such a reader.  Wait for a
grace period before returning success.  By then the page has left the
page cache, or is an order-0 folio with PG_hwpoison, which a reader that
starts after the grace period has to check.

Wait at the end, not right after setting PG_hwpoison.  A large folio is
split later, and until the new folios are in the page cache, a reader
can still copy the poisoned page as part of the large one.

Both callers hold mf_mutex and can wait for the grace period.

Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

+/* A page waiting for a grace period is not in the buddy yet: free it */
+static void mf_free_rcu_pages(struct page *p)
+{
+	if (!zone_rcu_free_waiting(page_zone(p)))
+		return;
+	if (page_count(p))
+		return;
+	if (is_free_buddy_page(p))
+		return;
+
+	rcu_free_sync();
+}
+

 		ret = __get_hwpoison_page(p, flags);
 		if (!ret) {
@@ -2440,6 +2454,7 @@ int memory_failure(unsigned long pfn, int flags)

 		goto unlock_mutex;

 	if (TestSetPageHWPoison(p)) {
 		res = -EHWPOISON;
 		if (flags & MF_ACTION_REQUIRED)
@@ -2447,6 +2462,13 @@ int memory_failure(unsigned long pfn, int flags)
 		if (flags & MF_COUNT_INCREASED)
 			put_page(p);

+		/*
+		 * The memory_failure() that set PG_hwpoison may have failed

+		 * unmap the page once this returns 0.

+			synchronize_rcu();
 		goto unlock_mutex;
 	}
 
@@ -2612,6 +2634,13 @@ int memory_failure(unsigned long pfn, int flags)

 	mutex_unlock(&mf_mutex);

Last comment 2026-11-02 [PATCH] x86/mm: Define the zapped-entry checks only with CONFIG_DEBUG_VM Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the x86 Linux kernel to only define and check for 'zapped-entry' conditions when CONFIG_DEBUG_VM is enabled. This change optimizes performance by reducing branch mispredictions in certain scenarios, such as zapping pages or using MADV_DONTNEED on large memory ranges. The patch introduces conditional compilation directives to control the definition of arch_check_zapped_pte and related functions based on the presence of CONFIG_DEBUG_VM.

This patch removes the zapped-entry checks in x86/mm, which are only implemented on x86 and warn about shadow stack entries in VMAs without VM_SHADOW_STACK. The checks are removed to improve performance by reducing branch mispredictions.

Last comment 2026-11-02 Re: [PATCH] mm/vma: read anon_vma carefully in validate_mm() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch adds a new function, `validate_mm_anon_rmap`, to the Linux kernel's memory management code. The function is responsible for verifying the consistency of anonymous memory mappings in a given VM area. It does this by locking the anon_vma and iterating over its chain, calling `anon_rmap_tree_verify` on each entry. This patch also removes unnecessary conditional compilation directives and moves the existing logic into a new function. The goal of this patch is to improve the robustness of the kernel's memory management code by catching potential issues with anonymous memory mappings.

Signals: requested changes
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) ↳ replying to David (Arm) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing David's feedback that the patch should assert whether a folio is uptodate in set_pte_range() and do_set_pmd(). The author agrees that rejecting the mapping is better than asserting, and instead proposes to add a function file_folio_not_uptodate() that checks if a folio is uptodate. This function will be called from finish_fault(), do_set_pmd(), and other places where it's necessary. The author provides a patch with compile-tested changes.
Show original comment 
Yes, rejecting sounds better.

Not in set_pte_range() as I suggested, though. Fault-around only gets
there with folios that next_uptodate_folio() checked under the folio
lock, and for CoW it maps the new anon folio after do_cow_fault() has
already copied from the page cache one.

Checking the folio ->fault() returned in finish_fault() covers PTE, PMD
and CoW; do_set_pmd() covers khugepaged. Both return VM_FAULT_SIGBUS.

Something like this, only compile-tested:

diff --git a/mm/memory.c b/mm/memory.c
index 6fe0b7dab927..ff2f2deff761 100644
--- a/mm/memory.c
+++ b/mm/memory.c
@@ -5674,6 +5674,22 @@ static vm_fault_t __do_fault(struct vm_fault *vmf)
        return ret;
 }

+/*
+ * A page cache folio that is not uptodate holds data that was never read in
+ * or cleared, and mapping it leaks stale memory to userspace. The callers are
+ * expected to check, so treat a hit as a kernel bug and refuse the mapping.
+ */
+static bool file_folio_not_uptodate(struct folio *folio)
+{
+       if (likely(folio_test_uptodate(folio)))
+               return false;
+       /* Driver pages from ->fault() never get PG_uptodate */
+       if (folio_test_anon(folio) || !folio->mapping)
+               return false;
+       WARN_ONCE(1, "refusing to map a page cache folio that is not uptodate\n");
+       return true;
+}
+
 #ifdef CONFIG_TRANSPARENT_HUGEPAGE
 static void deposit_prealloc_pte(struct vm_fault *vmf)
 {
@@ -5714,6 +5730,9 @@ vm_fault_t do_set_pmd(struct vm_fault *vmf, struct folio *folio, struct page *pa
                return ret;
        page = &folio->page;

+       if (file_folio_not_uptodate(folio))
+               return VM_FAULT_SIGBUS;
+
        /*
         * Just backoff if any subpage of a THP is corrupted otherwise
         * the corrupted page may mapped by PMD silently to escape the
@@ -5849,6 +5868,16 @@ vm_fault_t finish_fault(struct vm_fault *vmf)
        unsigned long addr;
        bool needs_fallback = false;

+       /*
+        * Check the folio ->fault() returned. For CoW that is the source of the
+        * copy; DAX CoW has none.
+        */
+       if (vmf->page) {
+               folio = page_folio(vmf->page);
+               if (file_folio_not_uptodate(folio))
+                       return VM_FAULT_SIGBUS;
+       }
+
 fallback:
        addr = vmf->address;
-- 
  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

Signals: agreement, request
Pedro Falcato ↳ replying to David (Arm) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Pedro Falcato expressed concerns about the fragility of page table manipulation APIs and usage in the kernel, specifically highlighting instances where it's possible to perform operations without holding necessary locks or with incorrect folio states. He suggested adding basic assertions (initially VM_WARN_ON_ONCE) to prevent such issues.
Show original comment 
Yes. FWIW, this is something that's been going through my mind. Our page table
manipulation APIs (and usage) are way too fragile and allow you to do the
most insane things without ever realizing it (e.g you can clear a pte, set
over a PTE, get-and-clear and discard the result, and do all of the above with
no locks held, set a pte for a folio that's not uptodate, set a pte for a folio
for which you don't have the lock, etc).

Some basic assertions (even if there's no easy way to back down from those)
would be nice, or ideally yes a rejection.

I would just add that these very much should be VM_WARN_ON_ONCE instead of
pr_warn_once(). pr_warn_once() will (AIUI) not be caught by fuzzers, at least
not as easily.

Also, if they're inexpensive enough, we might want to upgrade them to full
WARN_ON_ONCE().

-- 
Pedro

Neutral LLM

This patch series fixes two bugs in the swap code, allowing swapcache folios to be written out without anon mappings and ensuring cluster tables are not published until allocator locks are reacquired.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Nhat Pham Neutral LLM
The patch series addresses two bugs in the swap code and includes a second patch that completes Kairui Song's locking sketch. However, no technical concerns or suggestions have been raised yet.
Show original comment 
On Fri, Nov 6, 2026 at 9:04 AM Nhat Pham <nphamcs@gmail.com> wrote:
>
> Under memory pressure, swap_cluster_populate() can publish a table
> while its locks are dropped for a sleeping allocation. A cached
> allocator can consume and free that table before the populating
> allocator resumes, leaving the resumed scan with a NULL table.
>
>     CPU0                           CPU1
>     swap_cluster_populate()
>       drop local/global/ci locks
>       allocate and publish table
>                                    swap_alloc_fast()
>                                      consume the table
>                                    charge failure cleanup
>                                      free the table
>       reacquire local/global/ci
>       return ci
>     alloc_swap_scan_cluster()
>       read the missing table
>
> Keep the count table, cgroup table and zero bitmap private until
> swap_cluster_alloc_table() restores all allocator locks. Install them
> under the cluster lock and retain it through the scan. Initial bad-slot
> setup takes only the cluster lock because the device is unpublished.
>
> A stale reclaimer can return the isolated cluster to allocation while
> its locks are dropped. Abandon installation if the list state changes or
> a table appears, freeing only private allocations. Restore locks on
> allocation failure too, so the caller can return a still-isolated
> cluster to its free list.
>
> Reported-by: Sashiko <sashiko-bot@kernel.org>
> Link: https://lore.kernel.org/all/sashiko-outbox-181084@kernel.org/
> Link: https://lore.kernel.org/all/sashiko-outbox-181086@kernel.org/
> Co-developed-by: Kairui Song <ryncsn@gmail.com>

I think I need a

Signed-off-by: Kairui Song <ryncsn@gmail.com>

here as well...

This patch makes the zsmalloc shrinker compaction budget-aware by adding a check to ensure that at least one page can be freed before proceeding, addressing internal fragmentation concerns and potentially reducing the need for CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER.

5 reviewer comments — 0 participants

Chris Wiseman ↳ replying to Nhat Pham Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that the precheck for freeing zspage is a best-effort hint due to non-atomic counters, and suggested sending a patch to address this issue. Reviewer noted that compaction still provides benefits when triggered between bursts, but wastes time running mid-swap when new writes fill up freed slots, and suggested defaulting CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER to N (or CONFIG_ZSWAP) for a couple of releases
Show original comment 
Yeah, it just checks if a bucket has enough wasted slots to free at
least one full page before taking the locks.  Sergey pointed out those
two counters aren't atomic together so an unlocked check is just a
best-effort hint - if you and Sergey are good with that, happy to send a
patch.

---

Compaction still helps when we trigger it between bursts (freed ~21 MB
before the swap storm and ~10 MB after).  It just wastes time running
mid-swap when new writes are filling those open slots right back up.
Defaulting CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER to N (or CONFIG_ZSWAP) for a couple
releases makes sense to me.

Sergey Senozhatsky ↳ replying to Nhat Pham Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer suggested an alternative approach to implementing lockless best effort hint by introducing a ->can_compact bool flag, which would be set under the lock in zs_free(), and proposed reducing the overhead of calling zs_can_compact() on every zs_free() Reviewer noted that the dependency on ZSWAP is problematic because it introduces a runtime knob that needs to be tied in with the zsmalloc shrinker, and requested input from the zswap developers on their preferences for this configuration.
Show original comment 
Well, I guess... I don't mind the lockless best effort hint.

I suppose another way could be an introduction of ->can_compact bool flag,
which we can set under the lock in zs_free() (that's where fragmentation is
created).  zs_can_compact() doesn't seem too expensive, but calling it on
every zs_free() looks a bit bad, we sort of want to do less work in zs_free().

---

So "depends on ZSWAP" looks a bit problematic, because zswap has both
ZSWAP_SHRINKER_DEFAULT_ON (which can select CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER),
but then zswap also has module_param_named(shrinker_enabled).  So if
want to tie zsmalloc to zswap shrinker then we probably need to wire
in that run-time knob as well.  If shrinker_enabled should imply zsmalloc
shrinker (which I think it didn't so far).  zswap folks, do you have any
preferences?
View on lore ↗
Signals: alternative approach, reducing overhead, requested changes

created).  zs_can_compact() doesn't seem too expensive, but calling it on
every zs_free() looks a bit bad, we sort of want to do less work in zs_free().

> >
> > > Ah ok hmm. Sounds like internal compaction isn't that helpful, i.e
> > > zsmalloc already does a pretty decent job at packing objects that
> > > there aren't a terrible amount of internal fragmentation in the first
> > > place...?
> > > Should we do CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER (default to N or to
> > > CONFIG_ZSWAP) for now, and remove it in a couple of releases if users
> > > in the wild do not complain?
> >
> > Compaction still helps when we trigger it between bursts (freed ~21 MB
> > before the swap storm and ~10 MB after).  It just wastes time running
> > mid-swap when new writes are filling those open slots right back up.
> > Defaulting CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER to N (or CONFIG_ZSWAP) for a couple
> > releases makes sense to me.
> 
> SGTM, but I'll let Sergey chime in!

So "depends on ZSWAP" looks a bit problematic, because zswap has both
ZSWAP_SHRINKER_DEFAULT_ON (which can select CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER),
but then zswap also has module_param_named(shrinker_enabled).  So if
want to tie zsmalloc to zswap shrinker then we probably need to wire
in that run-time knob as well.  If shrinker_enabled should imply zsmalloc
shrinker (which I think it didn't so far).  zswap folks, do you have any
preferences?
Signals: NEEDS_WORK

Last comment 2026-11-05 Re: [RFC PATCH 0/2] mm: zsmalloc: make shrinker compaction budget-aware Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch proposes to make the ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER shrinker compaction budget-aware by defaulting its value to ZSWAP_DEFAULT_ON. This change is motivated by the observation that zswap-in (zswap_load) can cause fragmentation, and the additional shrinker trigger is unnecessary when zswap is disabled.

Signals: requested changes
Chris Wiseman ↳ replying to Yosry Ahmed Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Chris Wiseman noted that the zsmalloc shrinker is unnecessary when new pages are swapping out, as zswap naturally refills freed slots on its own, and suggested defaulting CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER to ZSWAP_DEFAULT_ON.
Show original comment 
I ran a quick test with zswap turned on to check.

Yosry is right - as long as new pages are swapping out, zswap naturally
refills the freed slots on its own.  Both runs ended up at the exact
same 95 MB pool size, and turning the zsmalloc shrinker off cut page
fault latency by 26% because reclaim isn't fighting itself for the same
locks.

The only time the shrinker actually saved memory in zswap (~20.8 MB) was
when I blocked new swap-outs completely after punching holes, since
zswap doesn't have a manual sysfs compact knob like zram.

ARM64 QEMU (Linux 7.3.0+, 4 vCPUs, zswap lz4):
                             Shrinker ON    Shrinker OFF
Active swap-out pool size:      95.9 MB         95.1 MB
Active swap-out p50 fault:     3,262 us        2,414 us  (-26%)
Idle pool (swap-out blocked):   44.2 MB         65.1 MB  (+20.8 MB)

Maybe we go with Nhat's idea (CONFIG_ZSMALLOC_SHRINKER defaulting to
ZSWAP_DEFAULT_ON) for now, and if zswap adds a proactive compact knob
later we can just kill the shrinker?
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Yosry Ahmed ↳ replying to Chris Wiseman Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Yosry Ahmed argued that the shrinker in zsmalloc is useless or harmful, and suggested dropping it if proactive compaction during reclaim via the shrinker is deemed ineffective; they also proposed treating proactive compaction as a separate feature to be evaluated independently
Show original comment 
Unless we lose something by dropping the shrinker that we can only get
back through proactive compaction, I would argue that adding the
proactive compaction knob (in zswap or zsmalloc) is orthogonal. If we
can establish that zsmalloc compaction during reclaim (via the
shrinker) is useless/harmful, that should be enough to drop it. Adding
a way to invoke proactive compaction should be a separate feature
judged based on its merits.

Patches Submitted (3)
[RFC PATCH v5 0/8] batch lookups in follow_page_mask() 8 patches
Show 8 individual patches
  • [1/8] mm/gup: break out gup_fill_pages() helper
  • [2/8] mm/gup: convert follow_page_mask() to return a long
  • [3/8] mm/gup: split out folio grab-and-store helper
  • [4/8] mm/gup: break out gup_lookup_pte() helper
  • [5/8] mm/gup: fill the pages array outside the pud/pmd lock
  • [6/8] mm/gup: return a huge page's full count from follow_page_mask()
  • [7/8] mm/gup: walk multiple PTEs per follow_page_pte() call
  • [8/8] mm/gup: batch contiguous same-folio PTEs into one refcount grab
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series optimizes the follow_page_mask() function by batching lookups in page tables, reducing cache maintenance and improving performance. The patches prepare for this change by refactoring the __get_user_pages() function and splitting the follow_page_pte() function into smaller helpers. The main changes occur in patches 7 and 8, which implement batched PTE walks and refcount batching for contiguous pages, respectively. These optimizations result in significant performance improvements, with a 2.3x to 5.9x increase in speed depending on the page size and system configuration.

🔥 TODAY [PATCH] x86/mce: Restore rate limit on MCE logged message Neutral LLM

This patch restores a rate limit on Machine Check Exception (MCE) logged messages, preventing a large number of messages from being printed to the console in a short period of time. The rate limit was inadvertently dropped in a previous commit, leading to potential issues with netcons infrastructure. This patch reinstates the original rate limit.

Last comment 2026-11-05 [PATCH] xfs: keep successful near rt allocations Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a performance issue in the XFS real-time allocation mechanism, which was causing unnecessary global searches for free space even when a nearby extent had already been found. The fix involves modifying the xfs_rtallocate_rtg function to only perform the global search if the initial near search did not find available space.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Carlos Maiolino ↳ replying to Rik van Riel Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Confirmed the correctness of the patch's approach to optimize XFS real-time allocation.
Show original comment 
On Wed, Nov 04, 2026 at 10:01:15AM -0500, Rik van Riel wrote:
> XFS realtime allocations first attempt to allocate space near where
> other parts of a file already are. If that allocation succeeds, it
> does not have to do a global search for free space.
> 
> Unfortunately, a code cleanup a while ago causes xfs_rtallocate_rtg
> to unconditionally do the global search, even when the search for a
> nearby extent has succeeded.
> 
> Fix it by running the global search only when the near search did
> not find available space.
> 
> Found by noticing higher kernel CPU use and examining the profile.
> 
> Fixes: fd048a1bb391d ("xfs: rework the rtalloc fallback handling")
> Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org # v6.12
> Reported-by: Christian Andres Saide <csaide@meta.com>
> Signed-off-by: Rik van Riel <riel@surriel.com>

This sounds correct yes.

Reviewed-by: Carlos Maiolino <cmaiolino@redhat.com>

>  		}

>  				prod, &rtx);

Last comment 2026-11-03 Re: [PATCH 8/8] mm/gup: batch contiguous same-folio PTEs into one refcount grab Neutral LLM

This patch breaks out a new helper function from gup_fill_pages() to simplify code and improve maintainability. The current implementation has inconsistent behavior, as some callers check for page existence while others do not. By moving the cache flushes to __get_user_pages(), the patch aims to reduce complexity and potentially merge more helper functions between slow GUP and fast GUP.

I agree this patch by itself isn't super useful.

It's goal is to be able to have this code in one
spot, and not in multiple places in gup.c

I'll split out the array filling from the cache
flushing, so we can move the cache flushing out
from under the lock, and have GUP-fast and regular
GUP share the same helper.

It really doesn't look too bad. Here are all
the GUP callers that need a cache flush:
- kernel/events/uprobes.c (through get_user_pages_remote)
- /proc/pid/cmdline (through access_remote_vm)
- fs/proc/base.c (through access_remote_vm)
- kernel/trace/trace_events_user.c (pin_user_pages_remote)
- arch/x86/kernel/cpu/sgx/ioctl.c (get_user_pages ... urgh)
- kernel/ptrace.c (access_remote_vm)
- mm/process_vm_access.c (pin_user_pages_remote)
- fs/exec.c (pin_user_pages_remote)
- virt/kvm/async_pf.c (get_user_pages_remote)

In other words, if we change the SGX code to just
call get_user_pages_remote(), and then move the
cache flushing to get_user_pages_remote() and
pin_user_pages_remote() we should be all set.

Want me to fold that into this series, or send
it in as a small, separate prequel series?

As a separate series it would:
1) split out the gup_fill_pages() and gup_flush_pages()
   helpers
2) convert the SGX code to use get_user_pages_remote()
3) move the flush call up the stack into pin_user_pages_remote()
   and get_user_pages_remote()

Just let me know if you prefer that separate, or
folded in.

This patch breaks out a new helper function from gup_fill_pages() to simplify code and improve maintainability. The current implementation has inconsistent behavior, as some callers check for page existence while others do not.
